Virgin Atlantic — Cheap Short-Haul Europe in Business

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Business class hops across Europe for fewer points than most domestic economy awards

Picture this: you've just crossed the Atlantic on a long-haul award, touched down at London Heathrow, and now you need to get to Rome for the real adventure to begin. Most travelers at this point resign themselves to a cramped economy seat on a budget carrier, dragging luggage through a secondary terminal. But you? You're walking into the Virgin Atlantic clubhouse, sipping a Bloody Mary, and then settling into a proper business class seat for the 2.5-hour hop to Fiumicino. That's the magic of this redemption. Here's the math that should make your jaw drop. A business class seat on a short-haul European flight — London Heathrow to Rome, London to Athens, London to virtually any major European city — routinely retails for **$1,000 to $3,000 in cash**. Airlines know that business travelers on these routes are often on corporate accounts and will pay whatever it costs. The leisure traveler simply doesn't book business class on a two-hour flight because it feels absurd. And that's exactly the pricing inefficiency the ChooseFI community learns to exploit. Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club prices these short-haul European business class seats at a flat **17,500 miles one-way**. At the low end of that $1,000–$3,000 cash range, you're already extracting **5.7 cents per point** — well above what most programs deliver. At the high end, when those seats spike to $3,000 on peak travel dates, you're looking at a staggering **17.1 cents per point**. For context, most travel rewards experts consider 1.5–2 cents per point a solid redemption. This one can deliver *ten times* that. What makes this feel particularly right for the FI mindset is the positioning angle. This isn't just a standalone splurge — it's the finishing move on a transatlantic trip you've already largely funded with points. You've landed in London. You need to get to your final European destination. Instead of paying $200 for a budget airline seat that charges extra for your carry-on, you drop 17,500 Virgin miles and arrive refreshed, fed, and ready. The opportunity cost of those miles is low; the experiential upgrade is enormous. This is what travel rewards done right looks like: not flying business class because you're wealthy, but because you understood the system better than the airlines expected you to.

The core insight here is understanding how Virgin Atlantic prices its own metal versus partner awards — and then deliberately choosing the right one for the right job.

Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club is famous in the points community for two things. First, its partner award sweet spots: ANA First Class for as low as 55,000–60,000 miles one-way, Delta One at competitive rates. These are the headline acts. But quietly sitting in the same award chart is a genuinely underappreciated gem — Virgin's own short-haul European business class, priced at a flat 17,500 miles regardless of the specific route within that distance band.

Why does Virgin price it this way? Virgin Atlantic operates as a premium carrier and sets its award chart to reflect aspirational redemptions — the kind that make Flying Club membership feel worthwhile. For long-haul routes on their own metal, their pricing is competitive but not exceptional. For short-haul Europe, however, the fixed pricing creates an opportunity: a two-hour flight to Rome or Athens costs the same 17,500 miles as a cheaper, shorter hop, but those premium routes have cash prices that routinely spike into the thousands. The chart wasn't built to be a loophole — it's just that cash prices for short-haul business class in Europe are genuinely volatile and often extraordinary.

The fuel surcharge reality check. Here's where you need to pay close attention. Virgin Atlantic is known for passing fuel surcharges onto award redemptions on their own flights. This is the opposite of what happens on ANA or Delta partner awards booked through Flying Club (which carry zero surcharges). On a short-haul European route, those surcharges are real but typically far more manageable than on a transatlantic segment — often in the $50–$150 range rather than $500+. The CPP math still works strongly in your favor, but you should go in with eyes open: this is not a zero-cash-outlay redemption.

The transfer path is exceptional. This is where the ChooseFI community has a serious structural advantage. Virgin Atlantic Flying Club accepts transfers from essentially every major points currency:

Every major transferable currency gets you there at a 1:1 ratio with instant speed. That means if you're holding Chase points from your Sapphire Reserve or Preferred, Amex points from your Gold or Platinum, or Citi points from your Premier card, you have a direct, frictionless pipeline to 17,500 Virgin miles within minutes of finding availability. This is critical because, as you'll learn in the booking section, Virgin availability on its own short-haul European routes can be limited, and you want to be able to move fast when a seat opens up.

The booking window opens 331 days in advance — use it. Miles don't expire as long as you have account activity within 36 months, so there's no pressure to burn them before you're ready.

Before you touch a single website, clarify your mission: you're looking for Virgin Atlantic-operated business class availability on short-haul European routes departing from London Heathrow (LHR). This is not a search for partner award space — this is VS metal, VS availability, VS seats. Your most common target routes will be LHR → FCO (Rome), LHR → ATH (Athens), and similar European city pairs. Build your itinerary around where VS actually flies in Europe, not every possible destination.

Go to virginatlantic.com and use the award search tool. Select "Book with miles" and enter your route. Virgin shows its own availability directly here — you don't need a third-party search engine for this specific redemption. Search one-way rather than round-trip so you can see each direction independently. If your target dates show no availability, flex by ±3 days. Business class award seats on short-haul routes are typically released in small batches.

Open a second browser tab and search the same route and date on Google Flights or directly on virginatlantic.com for a revenue ticket. Note the cash price for business class. This is your CPP sanity check — if the cash price is $1,200 and you're spending 17,500 miles plus ~$100 in surcharges, you're at roughly 6.3 cents per point. If the cash price is $2,500, your CPP jumps dramatically. This step helps you prioritize which dates to target.

Log into your Virgin Atlantic Flying Club account. If you don't have 17,500 miles already, check your transferable points balances:

Do NOT transfer points until you have confirmed award availability on your target date. Transfers to Virgin are one-directional and cannot be reversed.

Once you've confirmed a seat is available, transfer your points from your chosen program. For Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt transfers, the miles land in your Flying Club account instantly. Refresh your Flying Club account, confirm the miles are there, and complete the booking before the seat disappears. Enter your payment details for the taxes and surcharges (typically $50–$150 on short-haul European routes).

If you're struggling to find seats close to your travel dates, shift your strategy to the booking window's leading edge. Virgin opens award availability 331 days in advance. Set a calendar reminder 331 days before your ideal travel date, log in at that exact time, and book the moment inventory releases. Peak-season seats to popular destinations like Rome and Athens move quickly.

After booking, screenshot your confirmation, note your booking reference, and verify the routing, cabin class, and surcharge amounts. Add the flight to your calendar with the Virgin Atlantic booking reference. Sign up for flight notifications.

Virgin Atlantic operates its own short-haul flights from London Heathrow to destinations across Europe including Amsterdam, Paris, Edinburgh, Geneva, and Athens. These are different from partner codeshare flights. The 17,500-point price applies specifically to Virgin Atlantic-operated short-haul business class. Partner flights (booked through Flying Club but operated by Delta, KLM, or Air France) use a different award chart and typically cost significantly more. When searching, filter for Virgin Atlantic flight numbers (VS prefix) to find the sweet spot pricing. The business class product on short-haul includes a dedicated cabin, lounge access at Heathrow, priority boarding, and meal service.

Card links may earn ChooseFI a commission. See our advertiser disclosure.

Let's walk through a real scenario that illustrates exactly what this redemption looks like in practice.

The Setup: You've just arrived at London Heathrow on a transatlantic award — perhaps an ANA First Class redemption through the very same Flying Club program. You have a few days in London, and now you need to get to Athens, Greece, where your Mediterranean adventure begins. You check flights: a business class seat on Virgin Atlantic's LHR → ATH route on your target date is priced at $2,200 cash.

The Redemption: You log into virginatlantic.com and find a business class award seat on that same flight: 17,500 Virgin miles + approximately $120 in taxes and fuel surcharges. You have 20,000 Citi ThankYou Points sitting in your ThankYou account from your Citi Premier card. You initiate the transfer — 1:1, instant — and within minutes, your Flying Club account reflects 17,500 miles (with 2,500 to spare for future use). You complete the booking.

The Experience: On the day of travel, you access the Virgin Clubhouse at Heathrow — one of the most celebrated airport lounges in the world. The cocktail bar, the à la carte dining, the spa treatments available before departure — this is the part that makes the ChooseFI community members shake their heads in disbelief when they first experience it. Then you board and settle into Virgin's short-haul business class: a proper dedicated seat, a full meal service, attentive cabin crew. For a two-and-a-half-hour flight, it's a genuinely elevated experience.

You land in Athens refreshed, fed, and without having spent thousands of dollars. That's the positioning flight done right — and it's what separates travelers who understand the travel rewards system from those who are still paying cash for European business class.

Do this: Use it as a positioning flight on a broader trip This redemption was practically designed for trip architecture. If you're already planning a transatlantic journey — coming into London on a long-haul award — the 17,500-mile European hop becomes the natural, high-value conclusion. You're not just booking a short flight; you're completing a fully points-funded journey across two continents. Think of this as the last domino.

Do this: Search 331 days out for peak season Virgin's booking window opens exactly 331 days before departure. For summer travel to Athens, Rome, or other peak European destinations, business class award inventory gets claimed early. Set a phone reminder 331 days before your ideal travel date. Log in that morning. The seats that seem impossible to find in the 60-day window are often available the moment the calendar rolls over.

Do this: Compare cash prices before transferring Not all dates are created equal. A Tuesday in November to Rome might retail for $800 in business class — still a reasonable 4.6 CPP — while the same route on a Friday in July might hit $2,800, delivering 17+ CPP. Run the cash price check on Google Flights before you commit your miles. Target the high-cash-price windows for maximum value extraction.

Avoid this: Transferring points before confirming availability This is the single most common mistake. Transferring miles to Virgin Atlantic is a one-way street. If you send 17,500 Chase points over and then discover the seat isn't actually available (or you misread the cabin class), those miles are stuck in Flying Club. Always — always — confirm the specific flight and cabin availability first, THEN transfer.

Avoid this: Confusing this with partner award bookings Virgin's zero-surcharge policy applies to partner airlines like ANA and Delta. On Virgin Atlantic-operated flights (which this redemption is), expect fuel surcharges of $50–$150. Budget for these. They don't destroy the CPP math, but they're a genuine cash cost you need to plan around.

Do this: Hold Virgin miles for multiple redemptions Because miles don't expire with account activity every 36 months, there's no reason to hoard and rush. Keep Flying Club active by earning miles through their shopping portal or partners, and wait for the right route and cash price moment to pounce.

This redemption is built for a specific type of ChooseFI traveler: the intermediate-to-advanced points strategist who thinks in itineraries, not individual flights.

The ideal candidate is someone who's already planning a trip that routes through London Heathrow — perhaps on a transatlantic award — and needs an elegant, high-value way to reach their final European destination. Solo travelers and couples benefit most here. At 17,500 miles per person, a couple needs 35,000 Virgin miles, which is completely achievable with a single transfer from a combined Chase or Amex balance.

Families should approach with caution. This redemption scores a 2/5 for family-friendliness for good reason. Multiplying 17,500 miles per person across four or more travelers creates a significant points requirement, and availability for multiple seats on the same flight is far from guaranteed. The fuel surcharges also multiply with passenger count. Families are better served by other redemption strategies.

This is NOT for beginners. The overall score of 2.7/5 and beginner-friendliness rating of 1/5 signal real complexity. If you're just starting your travel rewards journey, build your foundation with simpler redemptions first. But if you've got a year of points accumulation behind you and you're ready to think architecturally about trip planning, this sweet spot can be a revelatory experience.

This redemption is rated **Hard** — and that rating is earned honestly. The difficulty isn't in the math or the concept; it's in three very real execution challenges. **Challenge 1: Finding award availability.** Virgin Atlantic doesn't release enormous quantities of business class award seats on its own short-haul European flights. You may search a dozen dates before finding a seat. This is the primary friction point. The fix: use the 331-day advance booking window religiously, and be genuinely flexible on your travel dates by at least ±3 days. **Challenge 2: Navigating the Virgin Atlantic booking interface.** Compared to programs like Chase's travel portal, Flying Club's award search tool is clunkier and less intuitive. It occasionally shows availability that isn't actually bookable through to completion. The fix: search virginatlantic.com directly, be patient with the interface, and have your payment information ready to move quickly when a seat surfaces. **Challenge 3: Understanding the surcharge reality.** New points enthusiasts sometimes expect zero out-of-pocket on award bookings. Virgin's fuel surcharges on its own metal are real, and they'll surprise you if you're not expecting them. The fix: budget $50–$150 per person in cash costs as a baseline assumption. The good news: the **transfer path is as easy as it gets** in the points world. Instant 1:1 transfers from Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt mean that once you find availability, you can have miles in your account and a booking confirmed in under 10 minutes. If you're willing to invest the search time and approach with patience, this is one of those redemptions that fundamentally changes how you see the travel rewards system. The payoff — business class in Europe for 17,500 miles — is worth the effort.

This redemption works best as part of a larger trip. If you are flying transatlantic to or from Europe, route through London Heathrow. Then use the short-haul award to connect to your final European destination at a fraction of the cost of a separate ticket.

Log into VirginAtlantic.com and search for reward flights in Upper Class (business) on your desired European route. Look for VS-coded flights only. Ignore partner-operated results, which will price differently.

Midweek flights (Tuesday through Thursday) and shoulder season dates offer the best availability. Peak summer weekends and holiday periods are harder to find. Build flexibility into your schedule if possible.

Chase Ultimate Rewards transfers to Flying Club are instant, making Chase the preferred transfer partner for time-sensitive bookings. Amex Membership Rewards also transfers 1:1 but may take 1-2 days.

Complete the booking on VirginAtlantic.com. Your business class ticket includes access to the Virgin Atlantic Clubhouse at Heathrow, one of the best airport lounges in Europe. Arrive early and enjoy it as part of the experience.

Virgin Atlantic operates short-haul flights from London Heathrow to destinations including Amsterdam, Paris CDG, Edinburgh, Geneva, Athens, Tel Aviv, and several others. Routes change seasonally, so check VirginAtlantic.com for current schedules. The 17,500-point sweet spot applies to all Virgin Atlantic-operated short-haul business class flights.

Yes, Virgin Atlantic Flying Club prices awards as one-way, so you can book a single short-haul business class segment for 17,500 points. This makes it ideal for positioning flights where you only need one direction covered by points.

The flight itself is short, but the value extends beyond the seat. You get dedicated check-in, priority boarding, the Heathrow Clubhouse lounge (which is excellent), a meal service, and a more relaxed travel experience. At 17,500 points, the per-point value (3-5 cents) makes it a strong redemption even for a short flight.

Virgin Atlantic does add taxes and fees to award tickets, including some carrier-imposed surcharges. On short-haul European routes, total out-of-pocket costs typically run $50-$150 depending on the route and departure airport taxes. This is higher than some programs but still reasonable given the business class product.

The Virgin Atlantic short-haul European business class sweet spot is the perfect complement to a transatlantic award trip. At just 17,500 points, it costs less than most domestic economy awards while delivering business class service, Heathrow Clubhouse lounge access, and a stress-free connection to destinations across Europe. It is not the flashiest redemption in travel rewards, but it is one of the most practical: a building block that makes multi-city European itineraries accessible and affordable for the FI community.

PlayStation Unveils New Hardware With Seriously Steep Price Tag

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Gaming hardware really has come on leaps and bounds in recent years, with console players finally able to keep up with those on PC for the most part. As well as first-party releases, both Xbox and PlayStation have plenty of partners who bring out additional products for their consoles, and it's a much healthier ecosystem with quality products all over the place.

That said, no piece of hardware is arguably as important as the controller, which provides our only method of getting the characters in games to do what we want. Particularly if you're a fan of fast-paced shooters and other genres that require high precision, a good controller really can be the difference between life and virtual death.

With all of that in mind, a new release is about to hit the market that really could be the very best example of a controller anywhere, though be warned that it comes with a price tag to match those lofty ambitions.

As first reported by GameRant, official PlayStation partner Razer has revealed its most premium PS5 controller to date, the new Raiju V3 Pro. Made from magnesium alloy and with plenty of bells and whistles to keep things interesting, the controller is priced at a very hefty $349.99, though you do get a lot of mileage for that outlay.

In sharing the news of the release with potential players, Razer shared the following insight:

"Razer’s most ambitious officially licensed PlayStation® 5 controller to date, uniting premium materials, a one-of-a-kind design, and high-level performance in a single collector’s piece — debuting in a limited, individually serialized drop of 1,337 units. Razer, the leading global lifestyle brand for gamers, today announced the Razer Raiju V3 Pro Signature Edition, its flagship controller officially licensed for the PlayStation® 5 console, built for players who want the highest level of performance and craftsmanship that has defined Razer engineering.

"Introduced in 2016, the Raiju controller line has been refined over the past decade evolving into Razer’s most advanced PlayStation® controller ever. The Razer Raiju V3 Pro Signature Edition distills that decade of experience into a single form, balancing design, weight, and performance for players on the PlayStation® 5 console who refuse to compromise. Limited to an initial release of 1,337 individually serialized units, each Raiju V3 Pro Signature Edition is produced through a demanding, precision-driven process – from its magnesium alloy exoskeleton to its finely detailed anodized aluminum accessory tool."

So what's under the hood? In fairness to the price, you do get a lot of features and build quality guarantees that you simply wouldn't get with cheaper options. Those include:

The magnesium exoskeleton and glass touchpad are new additions to this latest version of the controller, and they should go one step further in elevating it above previous iterations.

The Razer Raiju V3 Pro will be available to purchase from November 10, though with the limited numbers available, you will want to move quickly if you do want to get one for yourself.

Worthing FC puts focus on mental health as supporters association partner with suicide prevention charity Olly’s Future

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The charity, founded by Worthing mum Ann Feloy, has been announced as Worthing FC Supporters Association's chosen charity of the year and a fundraising day was held on Saturday, October 31.

Nearly 4,000 fans attended the game against Sutton United at Woodside Road and representatives of Olly’s Future were there with collection buckets. The charity also received a split of the match-day raffle, bringing the total raised to £870.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

Ann set up the charity in memory of her son Oliver Hare, who took his own life on February 14, 2017, just before his 23rd birthday. Its work includes delivering training worldwide to equip people with the skills to spot signs of someone who may be thinking of suicide and have a conversation that could help save their life.

Ann said: “We're delighted Worthing FC has chosen Olly's Future as its charity of the year. My son Oliver loved playing all kinds of sports, from football and rugby to tennis and squash.

“It's a local club being bold enough to get behind the message that suicide prevention is everyone's business, and particularly football where the majority of spectators are men.

“This is what the recent The Royal Foundation of the Prince and Princess of Wales' campaign On Your Side, hosted by Jake Mills Hub of Hope and fronted by Harry Kane, looks like on the ground.

“We all know the facts that three quarters of suicides are by men and that suicide is the biggest killer of men under 50. Supporting our suicide prevention charity goes a long way to reaching that vital target audience and we'll be working with the club to ensure we can help save lives from suicide together.

“However, this comes at a time when the town is in shock about the closure of its Worthing Samaritans branch, which has left volunteers reeling. I've spoken to many in the past week, since they were told of the closure of their premises in an efficiency drive.

“Local volunteers tell me they are being asked to work from home, which they feel is not tenable as it's vital for their own well-being to be with colleagues, or else drive to Brighton to carry out their shifts.

“For every step forward in suicide prevention, there seems to be two steps back. The Samaritans are closing numerous premises around the country - Worthing is not alone.”

The Rebels will be working with Olly’s Future throughout the coming season and the supporters association, run by Natalie Lunn, will be donating some of the proceeds from away match coach travel to the charity.

Natalie said: “I work as a nurse and know how difficult it is to access care for physical health let alone mental health. So when we were looking for a charity to support, I went online and found Olly’s Future, which is based locally.

“I have sons myself and some of their formative years were during Covid and I know how difficult it can be for young people. So,it is great to be able to contribute to a charity doing such valuable work.”

The club produced an enamel badge, linking the Love and Light logo of Olly’s Future with the team, for the fundraising day and this proved popular with supporters.

James Davies, Olly’s Future chief executive, has followed non-league football most of his life and he was part of the fundraising team on the day.

He said: “For us it is excellent to have such a relationship with a club which shows how much it cares about the issue of suicide.

“You see more than 3,000 people gathered together in one space and this offers a chance to open up about issues which are concerning them. They are with friends and are experiencing a sense of belonging as they get together to support the team.

“Non-league teams may not have the resources and the large fan-base of the bigger clubs, but they are very obviously rooted in towns such as Worthing and play a major part in the community. Tickets are also affordable in ways that premiership attendance is not for many people.”

His own recent personal experience has shown how much going to watch a match can mean to people who might otherwise be feeling isolated.

James explained: “I recently lost my grandmother and my grandfather, aged 87, had been her carer in her last years. He has started going each week to watch both Portland United and Weymouth matches along with other family members, which stops him just being at home and alone with his thoughts, and when I went with him, he really did open up about his feelings.”

Hackers exploited iOS and Android flaws to infect phones

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Researchers say group may have been Kremlin-sponsored in search of diplomatic targets.

Hackers possibly linked to the Kremlin exploited previously patched iOS and Android flaws in an attempt to hack visitors of multiple websites, including one celebrating the 250th anniversary of the signing of the US Constitution, researchers said Wednesday.

One of the impersonated sites was america250.org, belonging to a non-partisan group of lawmakers, business executives, and at least one former member of the Trump administration. The attackers registered america250[.]holiday and used it as a reverse proxy to america250.org. The imposter website delivered content from the real one that added malicious JavaScript into it.

“We assess that the intrusion set may have deliberately selected this website due to its association with a high-profile national celebration, potentially leveraging the event as a pretext for invitation-themed spear-phishing campaigns,” researchers from security firm Sekoia said.

A second website, theowo[.]club, proxied the website Raffles of London OWO, a luxury hotel physically close to some of the UK’s most sensitive government institutions, including the Ministry of Defence and 10 Downing Street, the official residence and office of the British Prime Minister. Open source reporting indicates that Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy stayed there during a visit on October 14.

“By doing this watering hole [impersonating] the Raffles London at The OWO, the intrusion set may have sought to target diplomatic delegations and high-profile guests through targeted spear-phishing campaigns,” the researchers said.

The attackers also delivered their attacks through compromised websites—including a top Ukrainian gas production company—and a Ukrainian web analytics service that serves thousands of websites. Sekoia said its attribution to the Kremlin is based on the targets’ profiles and similarities to a previous attack attributed to APT29, one of the names the suspected Russian group is tracked under.

The hackers relied on three infection vectors: direct JavaScript injection into traffic from legitimate websites, proxy domains such as america250[.]holiday, and the supply-chain compromise of the Ukrainian web analytics service. In each case, the injected JavaScript was the same. During the first stage, the JavaScript profiled the visitor’s browser and device. The attackers then used that information to deliver one of three zero-click exploit chains, which work without requiring any interaction from the target.

One of the chains was DarkSword, an iOS exploit kit that Google researchers said customers of PARS Defense, a commercial surveillance vendor in Turkey, and at least two other threat actors have used. Sekoia said the more recent campaign used two other exploit chains that weren’t named.

All of the vulnerabilities exploited in the campaign were “n-days,” meaning they had already been patched and attacks worked only on outdated versions of iOS or Android.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Defined benefit (pension) versus defined contribution

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

That discount for early retirement looks pretty severe. My DW retired at 55 from a government job and got 65% of the full retirement age benefit. In her case FRA was 62 and the discount was 5%/year, thus 7 years early and 35% discount. Most Bogleheads would probably be saving well enough that they don't care to hang on till 65 or close to it. The 1.5 to 2% factor is pretty good though.

I think the 1.5%/year max COLA makes the defined benefit a bit unattractive compared to assets that stand a good chance of keeping up with inflation. US inflation has averaged about 3%/year in the fiat money era, so expect the purchasing power of the pension to erode. 30 years from now with the current debt trajectory who knows what an adequate COLA will be. When she would invest in bonds, favor TIPS.

Why Opendoor Stock Jumped 13.8% Today

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Opendoor jumped as a Democratic sweep pushed oil and Treasury yields lower, but this is a rate-sensitive stock with a model I'm still skeptical of.

Opendoor Technologies (OPEN +13.79%) stock finished Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026, up 13.8% after a Democratic sweep in the midterms set off a rally in housing stocks.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.44%) finished up 1.7% and 1.5%, respectively.

Democrats took both chambers of Congress on Tuesday night in a sweeping victory. The party has said that affordability is a major policy priority, including the cost of housing.

Beyond that, the results led oil prices lower as investors hoped for a quicker resolution to the Iran war now that Democrats control the legislature. That could help cool inflation, which in turn may mean long-term Treasury yields retreat -- exactly what the 10-year Treasury yield did today.

Opendoor is in the business of buying and selling homes, so the more active the housing market is, the better the company's sales. If yields move lower, mortgage rates follow suit, which typically means more buying and selling.

Opendoor also sits on a large, financed inventory. Higher rates eat into its bottom line as it is forced to pay more in interest.

The company reports Q3 results after the close on Thursday, with Wall Street looking for sales growth of about 17%. We'll see if they deliver.

Regardless, I'm still skeptical of the model long term. I'd be careful reading too much into today.

Investing in Your 20s – Take the Match, Open a Roth IRA, and Own Stocks

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Money you invest in your 20s has about 40 years to grow, so small amounts go far. Contribute enough to your 401(k) to get the full employer match, open a Roth IRA while your tax rate is low, and invest both accounts mostly in stocks through a target-date or broad stock index fund.

Starting early can matter more than the amount you put in, since money you invest at 25 gets 10 more years to compound than money you invest at 35.

A Roth IRA suits your 20s. You pay income tax on what you put in at today’s rate, and in retirement the money and its growth come out tax-free. That trade pays off most when your tax rate is low, and on a first salary it’s likely the lowest it will be in your career.

With about 40 years until retirement, you can hold mostly stocks, which swing more than bonds but have historically grown more. If the market drops 30% when you’re 25, every contribution buys shares for 30% less while prices stay down, and you won’t need to sell for decades. Keep buying through it.

Take the steps in this order. First, get the full match, the money your employer adds when you contribute, as soon as your plan allows. Build a starter emergency fund, a small cash cushion for surprise bills, at the same time. Next, pay off any high-interest debt, such as a credit card balance. Then, if your income is under the IRS’s ceiling for Roth contributions, open a Roth IRA and contribute up to the yearly limit, $7,500. Anything past that goes back into your 401(k).

In both accounts, pick a low-cost target-date fund named for the year you plan to retire. It holds mostly stocks while that year is far off and shifts toward bonds as it nears. A broad stock index fund, which owns small slices of hundreds or thousands of companies, works too.

At a hypothetical 7% average annual return, raising your contributions from 6% to 10% of a $50,000 salary adds $2,000 a year that grows to about $400,000 over 40 years. So each time your pay goes up, save another 1% or 2% of it until you and your employer together put in 15% of your pay.

To choose a firm for your Roth IRA, compare the best places to open an IRA.

Opinions expressed here are ours alone, not those of any advertiser. The offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us. That compensation may influence which products we cover and where and how they appear on a page – including the order in which they appear – but it does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions. SunCoke Energy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Debt paydown and rising cash flow give the company room to reward shareholders while maintaining operational momentum.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, SunCoke Energy (SXC -2.70%) announced that it expects to modestly exceed the high end of its full-year 2026 guidance for consolidated Adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. Reliable operational performance across both operating divisions and reduced debt levels bolster long-term capital balance.

Three key takeaways from management's discussion stand out for investors.

Management reached a settlement regarding a 2025 contract dispute with steel maker Algoma Steel. This resolution eliminates working capital friction and helps boost expected 2026 free cash flow, which is cash generated after funding capital expenses, to between $180 million and $185 million.

The Domestic Coke segment, which produces high-carbon coal fuel for steel production, continues to see steady structural demand. Solid customer contracts and completed spot market sales mean the division locked in its full operational capacity for the year.

The Industrial Services handling and logistics segment experienced elevated river and terminal throughput. Higher volumes and integrated operations from the Phoenix terminal acquisition drove results above previous full-year projections.

Operations run at full capacity across core facilities, and SunCoke Energy is capturing higher terminal throughput. Long-term customer contracts provide stable revenue while management monitors debt payoff and market demand.

Capital allocation stays focused on strengthening the balance sheet and rewarding shareholders. Paying down the revolving credit line, a flexible borrowing facility, remains the primary debt target alongside ongoing dividends.

Trump Has Shifted the U.S.-Pakistan Relationship

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

South Asia Brief: Trump Has Shifted the U.S.-Pakistan Relationship

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s South Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: A proposed liquefied natural gas project in Pakistan underscores close ties with the Trump administration, garment workers in Bangladesh protest for pay increases, and India’s opposition alliance rallies to call for the resignation of the chief election commissioner.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s South Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: A proposed liquefied natural gas project in Pakistan underscores close ties with the Trump administration, garment workers in Bangladesh protest for pay increases, and India’s opposition alliance rallies to call for the resignation of the chief election commissioner.

Since U.S. President Donald Trump returned to the White House last year, Pakistan has banked heavily on doing business with the United States. With the Democrats flipping both houses of Congress in the U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday, though, one has to wonder if Islamabad could come out as another loser.

For one thing, scrutiny of Trump’s business dealings will certainly increase, constraining his administration’s capacity to pursue deals abroad.

A big topic of discussion in Pakistan this week—along with the midterms—is the news that a consortium of U.S. companies has proposed a $4.4 billion liquefied natural gas (LNG) project in Port Qasim, near Karachi. It would be Pakistan’s first LNG storage facility, helping to shield the country from global energy shocks.

The proposal entails a large-scale storage terminal and eventually a 680-mile delivery pipeline. The plan is backed by the U.S. Trade and Development Agency, U.S. International Development Finance Corp., and the Export-Import Bank of the United States.

Washington’s support for the proposed LNG project underscores just how much the U.S.-Pakistan relationship has changed in Trump’s second term. Bilateral ties were downgraded after the U.S. withdrawal from Afghanistan in 2021, in large part because Washington had long viewed relations with Islamabad through a security lens.

The Trump administration turned this approach on its head. The United States now prioritizes ties with Pakistan, and Trump often praises the country’s leadership. Though counterterrorism still figures in the relationship, Washington has emphasized economic and commercial issues in its engagement with Islamabad.

In particular, the United States has taken a strong interest in Pakistan’s critical minerals and cryptocurrency resources. It has pushed for the country to import more U.S. goods, including energy supplies. In October 2025, Pakistan received its first-ever shipment of U.S. crude oil. Washington has pushed to remove trade obstacles with Islamabad, including ending a two-year ban on U.S. soybean exports.

Pakistan, which had long pitched its economic offerings to the United States unsuccessfully, has capitalized on the Trump administration’s newfound interest in its assets. The Financial Times reported last year that Islamabad went as far as to offer Washington the opportunity to build and manage a port on the Arabian Sea. (Pakistan publicly denies this.)

The proposed LNG project would be one of the Trump administration’s most ambitious commercial ventures in Pakistan, perhaps putting an exclamation point on the shift in the U.S. approach to the country. Yet that shift might not even be sustainable—especially given the midterm results.

The Trump administration’s commercial focus is largely driven by the president’s whims and by the economic interests of his family members and close associates. But this arrangement won’t outlast Trump’s term, which ends in two years. Further, with Congress in the hands of the Democrats starting in January, it could also become more difficult to pull off.

Conversations with Pakistani interlocutors this week revealed a combination of curiosity and concern about ties with the United States in the Trump era. There seems to be a desire to understand how the relationship improved so quickly alongside a sense that it might not last for long, given Trump’s unpredictability. The Democrats’ victory will only sharpen these concerns.

These worries reflect an evergreen challenge for the U.S.-Pakistan relationship: how to craft a strong partnership that can withstand political change in both countries and weather external shocks.

The last time the White House officially announced a policy on Pakistan was during Trump’s first term, in August 2017, when a new South Asia strategy included a hard-line approach toward Islamabad. It’s clear that the United States is overdue in conducting a formal review of its Pakistan policy, even as the relationship is being informally reevaluated and refashioned.

Garment protests in Bangladesh. Garment workers have taken to the streets in industrial areas of Dhaka, Bangladesh’s capital, in recent days to call for pay increases. Clashes with security forces have prompted the closure of dozens of garment factories and raised concerns about shipment delays.

Garment workers are a critical constituency in Bangladesh, numbering around 4 million, and the sector accounts for 80 percent of the country’s export earnings. Bangladesh’s ready-made garment industry is a global success story; it made the country one of South Asia’s best-performing economies until a slump in the last few years.

With the downturn, Bangladesh seems to be paying the price for relying so heavily on a single export—as globally competitive as it was—for so long. The protests are just the latest policy challenge for Dhaka, which is already struggling with economic stress, energy shortages, and the world’s most serious measles crisis.

These issues risk distracting Bangladesh from pursuing economic and governance reforms—something the government, which took office in February, was given a mandate to prioritize.

India’s opposition alliance rallies. On Sunday, the main opposition alliance in India staged protests in New Delhi to call for the resignation of India’s chief election commissioner, Gyanesh Kumar. The rally drew tens of thousands of people and marked the latest mobilization against a voter roll revision that critics say favored the ruling Bharatiya Janata Party.

The youth-led Cockroach Janta Party has spearheaded the protests calling for Kumar to step down. This summer, the Cockroach movement galvanized protests over leaked exam papers that ultimately resulted in the resignation of the country’s education minister.

Now, India’s opposition is riding on the coattails of this activism. The success of the Cockroach movement is a gift to the opposition, which has long been hobbled by the absence of a popular leader and disagreements within its alliance.

Heat stress becomes serious vulnerability. The International Labor Organization (ILO) released studies last week warning that by 2040, workplace heat stress spawned by rising temperatures could lead to reduced working time equivalent to around 145,000 full-time jobs in Sri Lanka and 2,400 in the Maldives.

The reports estimate that these losses will be valued at around $26 billion in Sri Lanka and $576 million in the Maldives. The hardest-hit sector in Sri Lanka will be agriculture, along with services, manufacturing, and construction. In the Maldives, services and construction will be the most vulnerable industries.

The ILO findings are a reminder of the high economic cost of climate change in a region where economies are fragile—and where some of the most labor-dependent sectors are especially vulnerable to climate effects.

Last Sunday, around 200,000 people converged on the Bhutanese town of Gelephu to watch volunteers construct 108 stupas. (The number 108 is sacred in Buddhism.) The initiative is part of a campaign led by Bhutan’s king to transform Gelephu, near Bhutan’s border with India, into a “mindfulness city.”

Bhutan hopes to use the initiative, which emphasizes ecological sustainability and happiness, as an antidote to the country’s deepening economic stress. The stupa project is certainly an unorthodox way to tackle these problems. Critics will likely say it does little to reverse rising unemployment and a rapid outflow of highly skilled workers.

However, the project aligns with a long-standing Bhutanese principle known as “gross national happiness,” which measures national well-being not by income or other economic indicators but instead by the level of contentment among citizens.

Michael Kugelman is the writer of Foreign Policy’s weekly South Asia Brief and a senior fellow for South Asia at the Atlantic Council. An analyst of the region for nearly two decades, he previously directed the South Asia Institute at the Wilson Center. X: @michaelkugelman

Why Wayfair Stock Is Up Today

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Shares of Wayfair (W +10.99%) leaped 11% on Wednesday after the home goods-focused e-commerce leader delivered stronger-than-expected profits despite a tepid overall economic environment.

Wayfair's net revenue rose 9.7% year over year to $3.4 billion in the third quarter.

The gains were fueled by an 11.4% jump in U.S. net revenue to $3 billion, marking the online shopping platform's fastest pace of growth since the early stages of the pandemic.

"At the start of this year, we told you we expected to take share in the U.S. at a pace we had not seen in several years," CEO Niraj Shah said. "With U.S. revenue growing double digits against a roughly flat category here in Q3, we've done exactly that."

Wayfair's active customers grew 4.7% to 22.2 million as of Sept. 30.

Although average order value declined to $311 from $317 the prior-year quarter, customers are ordering more often and spending more overall.

Orders delivered in the third quarter jumped 12.2% to 11 million, while last 12-month net revenue per active customer increased 3.1% to $596.

All told, Wayfair's adjusted earnings per share surged 33% to $0.93. That was well above Wall Street's estimates, which had called for per-share profits of $0.79.

Looking ahead, Wayfair sees "high single-digit growth" in revenue in the fourth quarter.

"The momentum we have on orders and customers gives us confidence that our share gains are structural, and we are still in the early innings," Shah said.

AMC Theatres Sets All-Time Record for Quarterly Revenue, Thanks to ‘Spider-Man: Brand New Day’ and ‘The Odyssey’

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The blockbuster success of “Spider-Man: Brand New Day” and “The Odyssey” fueled a record-breaking quarter for AMC Theatres. Total revenue for the three-month period ended Sept. 30 hit $1.71 billion, up 31.6% from $1.3 billion a year earlier.

Those marked the largest returns ever posted by the world’s largest cinema chain, eclipsing the previous all-time high established in the second quarter of 2026, led by “Project Hail Mary and “Michael.” Prior to this year’s rebound, AMC’s quarterly revenue high-water mark was pro-COVID, set in the second quarter of 2019, when “Avengers: Endgame” was released and shattered any and all box office expectations. Related Stories Netflix Steampunk Western 'Bass x Machina' Is a Crackling Mix of Fantasy and Reality: TV Review 'Klara and the Sun' Review: There's No Life — Intelligent, Artificial or Otherwise — in Taika Waititi's Calamitous Android Fantasy

“In recent years, where other key players in our industry collapsed, at AMC we repeatedly instead have talked about recovery. Today, we can finally say that AMC has recovered,” AMC CEO Adam Aron said in a statement. “To our studio partners, lenders, business partners, employees and our millions of passionate shareholders, thank you for standing with us as we traveled through a long, and at times brutal, road. Mere survival was never the goal. Our goal ultimately was to thrive, and that is exactly and precisely where our focus now lies. We are highly energized by the tremendous opportunities ahead.”

Aron attributed the better-than-expected quarter to several factors, including the rising popularity of premium large formats, the A-List subscription service and a resurgent box office. This summer was the best since COVID, as “Spider-Man: Brand New Day” became the third-highest grossing movie in history with $2.5 billion and Nolan’s ‘The Odyssey” powered to an astounding $1.8 billion. Overall domestic theatrical revenue has already eclipsed $8 billion, a milestone the industry didn’t cross last year until December. With potential juggernauts like “Avengers: Doomsday,” “Dune: Part III” and “The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” still to come on the schedule, this year will easily be the biggest at the box office in post-pandemic times.

Now when people are going to the movies, they’ve been more likely to indulge in popcorn and soda. AMC’s food and beverage revenue hit $589.5 million for the three-month period, up 30% from $451.8 million a year earlier. Meanwhile, ticket revenue rose to $954.5 million, a 33% increase from the $715.1 million a year earlier.

Losses also shrank for AMC. The company’s net loss was $109.3 million, compared with $298.2 million in losses in the third quarter of 2025. It logged a diluted loss per share of 12 cents, after reporting a loss of 58 cents in the prior-year quarter.

“Sometimes short but sweet is the best way to summarize performance,” Aron wrote in a five-paragraph statement. “We expect the remaining film slate to propel both the domestic and global box office to their highest full-year levels in seven years. We could not be more enthusiastic about AMC’s prospects as this year draws to a triumphant close.”

Durbin Is Retiring, Marshall Just Lost—Why The Bill Threatening Your Credit Card Rewards Isn’t Dead

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dick Durbin is retiring and Roger Marshall just lost his Senate seat. The two most prominent champions of the Credit Card Competition Act, the law intended to regulate credit card interchange which will mean far fewer rewards for consumers, will be gone in January. The bill threatening the economics of card rewards, and the airlines that depend on them, has support from President Trump and Vice President JD Vance was an original co-sponsor so it may come back with his presidential campaign. But the thing about this legislation is that it’s more useful as a way to raise campaign money from both retailers and banks than as something Congress actually passes. Departures of Durbin and Marshall haven’t made the Durbin-Marshall bill go away. Durbin identified Vermont Senator Peter Welch as his successor on the issue. The Airline Lobby Is Right To Worry In this week’s Airlines Confidential, Airlines for America CEO Chris Sununu warns that state initiatives interfering with card fees would eliminate points-funded travel. Banks buy miles because rewards help attract customers and their spending. By reducing margins on that spending, it’s less valuable to spend to encourage consumers to use the product. In markets where government has gotten involved in interchange rates we’ve seen: smaller initial bonuses higher annual fees annual caps on awards award chart devaluations (if each mile is worth less, an airline can still award one mile per dollar on spend despite receiving less money for it from the bank) It also means less revenue for the airline. In the third quarter, American Express represented all of Delta’s profit. American Airlines is barely breaking even even with several billion net from Citibank. We’d have much shakier airlines and fewer flights, and aspirational leisure destinations like Hawaii in particular would see less air service. What The Credit Card Competition Act Actually Does The current Senate bill, S. 3623, would require covered issuers with more than $100 billion in assets to enable at least two unaffiliated payment networks. At least one would have to be outside of Visa and Mastercard. Merchants get to choose the routing. That’s meant to put downward pressure on merchant payment costs by changing who controls which network handles the transaction. Cards issued in a three-party system, such as American Express issuing its own cards, are exempt from the routing requirement. Merchants benefit from accepting cards. They get more sales, and higher average sales. It’s often less expensive to accept credit cards than to take cash (due to employee theft, incorrect change, higher insurance costs). Retailers want the service cards offer, but want the government to force Visa and Mastercard to provide it for less. Trump’s promise of $1,200 in annual family savings was completely made up and inconsistent with the bill. Currently a majority of interchange gets rebated to the customer. Regulation of interchange has never lowered prices to consumers. And card payments, funded in large part by interchange, provide services to consumers beyond just payments (and borrowings). They often fund consumer protections – not just stronger dispute resolution than debit cards, but also extended warranty, return protection, and travel protections. The Bill’s Strongest Value Is As A Threat Back in July 2023, I argued that this was an especially useful issue for opening contribution wallets on both sides. Retailers have a large, concentrated interest in lowering their acceptance costs. Banks, payment networks and airlines have a large, concentrated interest in preserving the existing economics. An unresolved legislative threat gives both sides a reason to pour dollars into campaigns and political committees. I’d still bet on another round of proposals, threatened amendments and lobbying. Durbin and Marshall are leaving but the retailers and banks with billions at stake aren’t. Politicians still want the money. The bill threatening the economics of card rewards, and the airlines that depend on them, has support from President Trump and Vice President JD Vance was an original co-sponsor so it may come back with his presidential campaign.

But the thing about this legislation is that it’s more useful as a way to raise campaign money from both retailers and banks than as something Congress actually passes. Departures of Durbin and Marshall haven’t made the Durbin-Marshall bill go away. Durbin identified Vermont Senator Peter Welch as his successor on the issue.

In this week’s Airlines Confidential, Airlines for America CEO Chris Sununu warns that state initiatives interfering with card fees would eliminate points-funded travel. Banks buy miles because rewards help attract customers and their spending. By reducing margins on that spending, it’s less valuable to spend to encourage consumers to use the product. In markets where government has gotten involved in interchange rates we’ve seen: smaller initial bonuses higher annual fees annual caps on awards award chart devaluations (if each mile is worth less, an airline can still award one mile per dollar on spend despite receiving less money for it from the bank) It also means less revenue for the airline. Year-end giving campaign launches for the AAN Research Program

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Support the urgent need for brain research funding

MINNEAPOLIS, Nov. 4, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- The need for brain research funding has never been more urgent. That is why the American Academy of Neurology (AAN) today announced its year-end giving campaign for the AAN Research Program, inviting people to support the researchers working to find new treatments and cures for brain diseases and disorders.

One hundred percent of every tax-deductible donation will go directly to brain research.

"Scientific breakthroughs begin with researchers who have the ideas and expertise to pursue important questions," said American Academy of Neurology President Natalia S. Rost, MD, MPH, FAAN, FAHA. "The AAN Research Program gives all of us the opportunity to help advance high-quality brain research, bringing hope to people affected by brain disease."

Brain research is what makes it possible to prevent, diagnose, treat, and eventually even cure conditions of the brain and nervous system. It also advances our understanding of memory, learning, sleep, mental health, and healthy aging, helping people live healthier lives.

The AAN Research Program has funded brain research for more than 30 years, owing to its success the exceptional pool of talented researchers in the AAN community, the world's largest association of neurologists and neuroscience professionals.

Demand for funding, however, continues to outpace available resources. Hundreds of researchers apply for AAN grants. The AAN invested nearly $1.8 million in 2026, but millions in viable neuroscience research went unfunded.

"We know that excellent research proposals go unfunded," said American Academy of Neurology Chief Executive Officer Mary E. Post, MBA, CAE. "We want to make sure that we have the funding to support the research that may eventually lead to a cure for MS or a way to prevent Alzheimer's. Every contribution can help researchers pursue discoveries as they work toward improving brain health for all."

With neurologists leading the AAN Board of Directors, Science Committee, and Research Program Subcommittee, the AAN is guided by trusted experts who understand both the science and the real-world application of research.

Help advance research by making a tax-deductible gift on the BrainHealth.com "Donate to Research" page. Organizations interested in supporting the AAN Research Program may contact donations@aan.com.

The AAN Research Program is administered through the American Academy of Neurology Institute, the Academy's 501(c)(3) charitable organization.

Discover more about brain health at BrainHealth.com, a free public resource from the American Academy of Neurology. It offers a website, podcast, and books, connecting you with trusted information from the world's leading experts in brain health. Follow BrainHealth.com on Facebook, X, and Instagram.

The American Academy of Neurology is the leading voice in brain health. As the world's largest association of neurologists and neuroscience professionals with more than 44,000 members, the AAN provides access to the latest news, science and research affecting neurology for patients, caregivers, physicians and professionals alike. The AAN's mission is to enhance member career fulfillment and promote brain health for all. A neurologist is a doctor who specializes in the diagnosis, care and treatment of brain, spinal cord and nervous system diseases such as Alzheimer's disease, stroke, concussion, epilepsy, Parkinson's disease, multiple sclerosis, headache and migraine.

Explore the latest in neurological disease and brain health, from the minds at the AAN at AAN.com or find us on Facebook, X, Instagram, LinkedIn, and YouTube.

How Much Premium Economy Costs On London-New York Flights In 2026

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Premium economy on a London-New York flight can cost more than twice as much as an economy ticket, but the difference between the two cabins and business class can sometimes be surprisingly small. For passengers facing an approximately eight-hour transatlantic journey, a wider seat, extra legroom, better meals, and extra baggage can be appealing, but does the experience justify the added cost?

In 2026, passengers traveling between London Heathrow Airport (LHR) and New York JFK Airport have several airlines to choose from, including British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, and JetBlue. Those flying to Newark Liberty International Airport (EWR) have fewer nonstop options, primarily British Airways and United Airlines. With different premium economy products, competing fares, and varying baggage allowances, how much should passengers actually expect to pay for a more comfortable transatlantic journey?

Premium economy on the London-New York route typically costs approximately $1,000 to $1,300 for a return journey, compared with around $500 to $600 for a standard economy ticket. This means passengers can expect to pay roughly twice as much for the upgraded cabin, although the difference depends on the airline, travel dates, departure airport, and availability.

According to our analysis of premium economy fares in 2026, the transatlantic market demonstrates how airlines position their intermediate cabin between standard economy and business class. Premium economy has become a major revenue opportunity for carriers, letting them charge more for added comfort without offering the considerably more expensive lie-flat seating associated with business class.

However, the difference between cabins isn't always consistent. A NerdWallet comparison of British Airways fares found that one evening departure from New York JFK to London offered premium economy for $1,162, more than twice the $532 economy fare. Business class was available for just $1,222, meaning passengers would have saved only $60 by selecting premium economy instead. Another comparison showed a return premium economy fare of $1,459 against $737 in economy. These examples raise an important question: how much competition exists between airlines on the route, and where can passengers find the best selection?

The greatest selection of nonstop services is available between London Heathrow and New York JFK. According to FlightConnections, five airlines serve the route: British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, and JetBlue. The route covers approximately 3,440 miles (5,536 kilometers), with flights generally taking around eight hours.

However, passengers should distinguish between the airlines operating the route and those offering a dedicated premium economy cabin. British Airways calls its intermediate product World Traveller Plus, Virgin Atlantic markets its cabin as Premium, and American Airlines offers Premium Economy on eligible long-haul aircraft. Delta also operates Premium Select on eligible international services. JetBlue, meanwhile, offers its Mint business class and Even More Space economy seating rather than a conventional dedicated premium economy cabin.

The situation is different for passengers traveling to Newark. According to FlightConnections' LHR-EWR route information, British Airways and United Airlines operate nonstop flights between the two airports: United's intermediate product corresponding to British Airways’ World Traveller Plus is called Premium Plus. The route covers approximately 3,454 miles (5,559 km), with an average flight time of seven hours and 55 minutes.

British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American, Delta, JetBlue

World Traveller Plus, Premium, Premium Economy, Premium Select

Such a variety of airlines at JFK makes it attractive to travelers seeking schedule flexibility and competitive fares. Nevertheless, more airline options do not automatically mean cheaper premium economy tickets. British Airways and Virgin Atlantic offer useful examples of what passengers can expect to pay.

A closer look at the price bump that is required to fly in BA's Club cabins.

British Airways and Virgin Atlantic are two of the most prominent competitors on the London-New York market, and both offer dedicated premium economy cabins on their long-haul services. Their current fares demonstrate that passengers departing London can sometimes find considerably more attractive prices than those booking an equivalent journey from the United States.

Virgin Atlantic's official website provides a particularly useful snapshot of its pricing. For a return journey from London Heathrow to New York JFK between November 17 and November 22, 2026, the airline advertised Premium fares starting at approximately $1,529. Its published offers for selected dates in May, July, and August 2027 also showed starting fares of approximately $1,542 to $1,549. These are advertised starting prices, not guaranteed fares for every departure.

The airline's official Premium flight deals page also provides information about its cabin and included services. British Airways, meanwhile, allows passengers to compare World Traveller Plus fares through its official website.

Furthermore, the KAYAK sample for February 5–12, 2027 provides another useful comparison point during low season, with prices slightly lower than those for immediate departures within two weeks, although those dates fall outside the 2026 calendar year. With the fares set, the next question is what passengers get for spending more than £1,000 on a return ticket.

One of the main advantages of premium economy is extra personal space, which can make a big difference on an overnight transatlantic journey or for passengers with health issues. British Airways' World Traveller Plus cabin features wider seats, additional legroom, greater recline, lumbar support, and adjustable headrests and footrests. Passengers also receive an enhanced meal service, complimentary bar service, personal entertainment systems, and priority boarding.

Virgin Atlantic's Premium cabin offers a similarly upgraded experience, although the precise seat specifications depend on the aircraft operating the flight. According to its official cabin information, the airline offers up to 38 inches of seat pitch, additional room to stretch out, and a footrest. Its onboard experience also includes restaurant-inspired meals served on porcelain dishware, a welcome glass of bubbly, and additional snacks on selected aircraft.

Baggage is another important consideration when calculating the actual cost difference between economy and premium economy. Virgin Atlantic Premium includes two checked bags weighing up to 50 pounds (23 kg) each, plus a 22-pound (10-kg) hand luggage allowance. British Airways World Traveller Plus also provides two checked bags on most routes, while some economy fares are hand-baggage-only tickets. According to NerdWallet, adding two checked bags to certain British Airways economy bookings can cost up to $175 each way.

These additional services make premium economy more than simply an upgraded seat. However, the cabin doesn't offer the same experience as business class, especially for sleeping arrangements and airport lounge access. This distinction becomes important when the price difference between the two cabins starts to narrow.

The price difference between premium economy and business class fares is not always huge. Airlines use dynamic pricing systems that adjust ticket prices according to demand, remaining inventory, booking patterns, and the number of seats available in each fare category. As a result, a premium economy ticket can occasionally approach the price of a discounted business class seat, especially when demand for the intermediate cabin is high.

NerdWallet’s British Airways example illustrates this well. One New York JFK departure offered premium economy for $1,162, compared with $532 for economy and $1,222 for business class. In this comparison, choosing premium economy instead of business class saved only $60. For passengers seeking the additional comfort of a fully flat bed on an overnight flight, that relatively small difference could make business class a more attractive option.

Passengers also have another option when considering British Airways: using Avios. According to Award Travel Finder's London-New York redemption analysis, a one-way off-peak World Traveller Plus redemption requires 46,750 Avios, increasing to 66,000 during peak periods. Economy requires 27,500 Avios off-peak and 33,000 during peak periods. Taxes and charges are additional, meaning passengers must compare the monetary cost of an award ticket with the cash fare before deciding whether redemption represents good value.

Available loyalty points and occasional business-class promotions make comparing all three cabins especially important. Nevertheless, for passengers without enough points or who can't find attractive business class fares, premium economy remains an interesting middle ground. The question is whether the added comfort justifies the higher cost on this transatlantic route.

Premium economy occupies an interesting niche in the London-New York market because it provides a noticeable improvement over standard economy, often without the substantial expense of business class. On an approximately eight-hour journey, additional legroom, wider seats, improved dining, and priority airport services can make a meaningful difference for some passengers.

However, the financial case weakens when the fare approaches business-class pricing. A passenger paying $1,162 for premium economy when business class costs $1,222 is arguably better off considering the higher cabin, provided the business class fare is genuinely available for the same itinerary and booking conditions. Conversely, Virgin Atlantic's advertised return fare of $1,529 for November 2026 demonstrates that premium economy can be considerably more accessible than some of the higher-priced examples found in the US market.

Competition also gives passengers some flexibility. Travelers to JFK can compare British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American Airlines, Delta, and JetBlue, while Newark offers British Airways and United as nonstop options. Checking both New York airports, comparing departure dates, and examining the differences between economy, premium economy, and business class can reveal opportunities that aren't immediately apparent when searching for a single airline.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

Premium economy isn't automatically worth twice the price of economy, but the cheapest available ticket isn't necessarily the best option. The deciding factor will be the actual fare difference on the selected dates, the baggage and onboard benefits included, and how much passengers value additional comfort during their transatlantic journey.

Pharming: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A rare-disease player with a stabilized core product is now targeting a market 26 times larger, fueled by cash generation that needs no external financing.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Pharming (PHAR -3.64%) announced total revenue grew 21% to $117.6 million. Operating profit increased 89% to $29.9 million as the business expanded sales of its primary rare disease treatments. Strong cash generation gives the business funding to expand its clinical drug pipeline without needing extra financing.

RUCONEST, a treatment for hereditary angioedema (HAE, a rare genetic condition causing severe swelling), returned to revenue growth after earlier quarterly declines. Management maintained over 90% of active patients despite new oral competition, stabilizing demand among high-volume users.

Joenja, a therapy for activated phosphoinositide 3-kinase delta syndrome (APDS, a rare primary immunodeficiency causing immune system dysfunction), expanded through new international launches and U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) pediatric approvals. International sales now generate roughly one-third of total Joenja revenue.

Management's advancing leniolisib into common variable immune deficiency (CVID, a primary immunodeficiency disorder affecting immune function), a market 26 times larger than APDS in patient prevalence. Positive phase 2 data in related primary immunodeficiencies (PIDs) provides proof of concept for the broader indication.

Operating profit is surging as commercial sales expand, and management isn't hiding its satisfaction. High patient retention and specialized manufacturing create durable competitive moats. Management is strategically watching channel inventory timing and pediatric adoption.

Disciplined execution and clinical expansion set up long-term growth across broader immunology markets.

The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Business Edition® Visa® Card with Absolute Rewards® Review – Flagstar’s No-Fee 1.5% Business Card

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Get the Business Edition® Visa® Card with Absolute Rewards® if you bank with Flagstar and want to move another bank’s card balance, since it gives 12 billing cycles at 0% on transfers and purchases. Without a balance to move, a no-fee card paying 2% on your first $50,000 a year beats this card’s 1.5% until your spending passes $100,000.

First National Bank of Omaha (FNBO) now issues Flagstar’s credit cards, and this is the rewards card in Flagstar’s business lineup, which no longer includes the old Visa Business Cash card. Flagstar’s other two business cards are a no-rewards card with 18 billing cycles at 0% and a secured card that earns 1 point per dollar.

It suits Flagstar customers paying down another bank’s card while earning on new purchases, since moving $10,000 at account opening costs a $500 fee and no interest through the first 12 billing cycles. Owners who pay in full earn more with the no-fee American Express Blue Business Cash Card until yearly spending passes $100,000, since it pays 2% on the first $50,000 a year and 1% after, against this card’s 1.5%.
