Prediction markets cement their status after forecasting Democratic sweep of Congress

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Prediction market platforms cemented their role in election forecasting on Tuesday, when markets accurately predicted a host of races before the polls closed, and projected outcomes before news outlets.

Democrats were seen as favored to flip both the U.S. House of Representatives and Senate on prediction markets before the first polls closed at 6:00 p.m. ET Tuesday, and were seen as narrow favorites to take at least 53 Senate seats, while a 54th was forecast as a toss-up.

So far, Democrats have won 54 Senate seats — including wins in Iowa, which prediction markets placed at 50-50 odds, and South Carolina, where Republicans narrowly favored. Outstanding results remain in Mississippi and Alaska, where prediction markets give Democrat Scott Colom and Mary Peltola 90% odds of winning.

Leading prediction market platforms were actively projecting races on X Tuesday night. Those projections, at times, came before many media outlets made their own projections, or before even a substantial number of votes had been counted.

Kalshi was confident making those calls, as long as odds for a candidate were above 99% and trading activity for the opposite candidate "dried out,"  said Jack Such, a spokesperson for the platform.

Polymarket celebrated on X how it projected races, saying that in 10 key races, the platform called races ahead of outlets including CNN, Fox News and the Associated Press by a median 31 minutes.

"It's now routinely hours between when prediction markets call it versus the TV networks," said Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan in an X post. "No knock to them, but now there's Polymarket, and many people are realizing they want to know what's happening in real time."

Even in races where markets initially favored the candidate who eventually lost, they quickly adjusted once initial vote counts came in. In South Carolina, Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews was seen as the favorite on Kalshi to win shortly after 7:30 p.m., only 30 minutes after polls closed. In Texas, where Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa ousted Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, Hinojosa's odds on Polymarket jumped from 39% to 73% within five minutes of initial poll closings.

"You now have financially motivated number crunchers doing this, around the world, that give you that signal just that much faster," said Flip Pidot, a member of the board of directors at the Coalition for Political Forecasting.

Still, a successful night for prediction markets shouldn't stop efforts aimed at stronger regulation of the platforms, said Michelle Kendler-Kretsch, a researcher at the Anti-Corruption Data Collective, a nonprofit. She cited a recent Reuters/Ipsos survey that found most Americans are against using prediction markets to trade on elections.

"Risks and vulnerabilities didn't change overnight just because the markets were accurate this time around," she said.

Right before the midterms, prediction market forecasts had missed major global elections, including Brazil's presidential race, where Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva only had about 20% odds to win on Kalshi before polls closed.

Prediction markets also whiffed in some high-profile Democratic primaries over the summer, forecasting extremely high odds for progressives in Wisconsin and Michigan, for example. The Michigan Democrat, Abdul El-Sayed, narrowly won his primary before going on to win Thursday's general election.

Academics who've researched prediction markets for decades say the platforms aren't wrong when candidates with low odds on markets end up winning races.

"If you toss two coins, there's a 25% chance that you'll throw two heads in a row, and nobody would be terribly surprised if you did," said Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College.

Despite their early success on Tuesday, Dartmouth College economic professor Eric Zitzewitz said it's still not clear if casual observers understand how market probabilities work.

"Some people who are coming to [prediction markets] for the first time will see 55 by 45 odds and think that, 'Oh the candidate with 55 is way ahead,'" he said.

As prediction markets gain traction, however, the public will improve at understanding how to interpret their odds, Zitzewitz said.

With another election under their belts, prediction markets will remain a force through the next presidential election, experts said.

"Last time around [in 2024], prediction markets were in the discussion, but they were in the discussion in a way that was a little bit unsure," said Harry Crane, a professor of statistics at Rutgers University. "This [was] the first true prediction markets election."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

After Training for My First Marathon, I Felt Like My Body Didn’t Look Like the Runner I Thought I’d Become. Experts Helped Me See Otherwise.

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Marathon training changed how I saw my body—even when it didn’t change how I looked.

Two weeks out from my first marathon, I looked in the mirror and didn’t see the version of myself I thought I would. There was no six-pack, not even a hint of ab definition. My glutes weren’t particularly sculpted, and I wouldn’t exactly describe myself as lean like many runners I see.

At 5-foot-10, I’ve never been the smallest girl in the room. And while my long legs certainly help me cover more distance, I expected months of marathon training to leave me looking more visibly athletic, with more muscle definition and a physique that reflected just how hard I’d been working. I thought that after months of early alarms, added protein, and steadily increasing mileage, I’d look in the mirror and see a different body type.

Somewhere along the way, I absorbed the idea that a runner’s body should look a certain way. When mine didn’t match that image, I started wondering if I wasn’t training hard enough, if I was doing something wrong, or if my body simply wasn’t responding the way it was supposed to.

As I approached race day, I thought about why I was holding myself to that standard in the first place, especially because I was running the New York City Marathon on behalf of Planned Parenthood of Greater New York, an organization whose work centers on bodily autonomy and compassionate care.

Why was I putting so much pressure on my body to look different when it was already doing something incredible? I realized training for a marathon isn’t just about building endurance; it can also mean confronting the expectations you place on yourself.

So many people have an idea of what a “runner’s body” is supposed to look like, particularly fed by what we see online. When you see the same body type represented over and over, it’s easy to start associating that appearance with athleticism. But running a marathon is an enormous physical undertaking, and there’s no single body type that proves you’re capable of doing it.

Clinical sport psychologist Hillary Cauthen, PsyD, explains that the concept of “a runner’s body” is “largely a socially constructed ideal influenced by media representation, sport culture, and the tendency to associate certain physical characteristics with athletic success.” She adds that distance running has historically been represented by a lean body type, particularly smaller-framed athletes, which can leave some recreational runners questioning whether they fit the mold.

Comparison is a large part of this problem, too, says Cindra Kamphoff, PhD, mental performance coach and founder of the Mentally Strong Institute. “We see elite runners and start believing that’s what we should look like,” she explains.

Marathon training is practically a master class in comparison, making it hard to avoid. You’re tracking your pace, watching your mileage climb, sharing runs on Strava, and seeing what everyone else is doing. It can feel like your body should be changing right alongside your stats.

There’s also a particularly frustrating point of tension for women, who can feel caught between the cultural expectation to be smaller and the athletic ideal of looking lean and strong. Carinthia Bank, LCSW, a social worker who works with runners on body image, describes this as the “female athlete paradox,” in which women are judged against both ideals.

For example, a woman might recognize that building strength and fueling adequately are essential to marathon training, yet still feel pressure to maintain a smaller frame or worry that eating more to support her mileage will change her body in ways she doesn’t want. These competing expectations can shape how women judge their bodies, even as they get stronger and more capable, Bank explains.

Marathon training can also increase the time we spend thinking about our bodies, particularly when we’re experiencing physical or emotional discomfort, Bank adds. Many runners tend to think they’d feel better if their bodies were different, which can lead to judgment and blame toward the body they have.

That’s the trap I found myself in. While I was training for 26.2 miles, part of me was still measuring my progress against an imaginary finish line where I’d emerge with a completely different-looking body. I would accomplish something I’d never done before, like running my farthest distance, yet I was using my appearance to dictate my progress.

Cauthen offers a simple definition to keep in mind: “A runner’s body is not a specific shape or size. It is a body that runs.”

Here’s the thing about marathon training: Your body adapts to the physical demands of running, but those changes don’t guarantee an aesthetic transformation. You might build muscle, experience weight fluctuations, or notice an increase in appetite as your body works to fuel and recover. “Improved fitness does not always result in weight loss or a smaller body,” Cauthen says.

There’s also a practical reason not to make getting smaller one of your goals in marathon training. Kamphoff encourages runners “to focus on fueling for performance rather than restricting for appearance,” reminding us that “you can’t expect your body to perform at its best if you’re constantly trying to make it take up less space.”

Marathon training requires substantial energy, and restricting food to change your appearance can leave you without enough fuel to support your workouts and recovery.

Plus, fixating on your size can affect more than physical performance. “Focusing on getting smaller can distract and discourage us from actions that are helpful to performance and mental health,” Bank says. When you’re preoccupied with changing your body, you can miss out on learning what helps you perform, enjoying the process, and appreciating everything your body allows you to do.

I had to confront the fact that I’d been treating my appearance as a report card for my training. If I was working hard enough, I assumed I’d eventually see visible proof in the mirror. But I realized I can become a stronger, more capable runner without looking the way I imagined I would.

For me, that meant recognizing that the body I’d been criticizing was the same one carrying me through early morning runs and increasingly long distances. My body wasn’t failing at becoming a runner’s body—I’d been expecting it to meet a standard that had very little to do with what I was asking it to do.

I wish I could say that learning all of this made me instantly love every inch of my body, but that wouldn’t be honest. I’m still working on it, and I still have moments when I catch my reflection and wonder why I don’t look the way I imagined I would after months of training. But I’m trying to shift my focus from what my body looks like to what it’s allowing me to do, with the help of these expert tips.

“One of the most important shifts runners can make is moving from body evaluation to body appreciation,” Cauthen says. Instead of asking whether I look like someone who runs marathons, I’m trying to ask myself what my body has helped me accomplish. And the answer is a lot more impressive than whether or not I have visible abs.

As I got closer to race day, I continued to remind myself that I don’t have to look a certain way to feel proud of what I accomplished. My body carried me through months of training, and that’s worth celebrating all on its own.

I’m also learning to give myself a little more grace when my expectations don’t match reality. Bank reminds runners that “human beings usually get uncomfortable when there’s a difference between what we wanted and what we get,” and encourages compassion rather than self-criticism.

I don’t have to ignore the fact that I expected marathon training to change my appearance, but I also don’t want those expectations to overshadow everything else I’ve gained, including running farther than I ever thought possible, learning how to fuel and maintain energy, and showing up for myself.

Cauthen recommends distinguishing between observing a physical change and assigning a negative meaning to it, which has really stuck with me. “Noticing that your legs have become more muscular, your weight has fluctuated, or your clothing fits differently is an observation,” she explains. “Deciding those changes mean you are too big, not fit enough, or don’t look like a runner is an interpretation.” This helped me realize how quickly I can turn a neutral observation into self-criticism.

Instead of jumping to conclusions, Cauthen says it’s helpful to ask yourself a few questions when those negative thoughts creep in. For example, “is this thought based on evidence or comparison?” and “am I evaluating my body based on appearance or how I feel and function?” These questions challenge the assumptions that we tend to make about our bodies.

Kamphoff encourages runners to develop what she calls an “inner coach” instead of listening to an inner critic. One strategy she recommends is to “capture your wins” by writing down three things you’re proud of after training. The idea is to shift your attention from perceived imperfections to the progress you’ve made.

I’m trying to practice this by celebrating the things that don’t show up in a mirror. For example, I’ve learned how to pace myself and keep going when a run gets uncomfortable. “Your body has been showing up for you mile after mile. Make sure your self-talk is showing up for your body, too,” says Kamphoff.

Ultimately, I’m learning that I don’t need to love how my body looks every single day to respect what it does for me. “Body acceptance does not require loving every part of your appearance every day,” Cauthen says. “Sometimes it starts with respecting your body’s needs and appreciating its efforts, even when your feelings about your appearance are complicated.”

I can have complicated feelings about the way I look and still be proud of the athlete I’ve become. And I can show up at the starting line without meeting some imaginary physical standard first.

That’s the mindset I carried with me to the marathon. I ran 26.2 miles for a cause I believe in, after months of training that challenged me physically and mentally. My body didn’t have to look a certain way to deserve that moment.

After all those early mornings and hours of preparation, I finally crossed the finish line, put the medal around my neck, and walked out of Central Park feeling something I hadn’t quite felt throughout training: pride. Not because my body looked different, but because of everything it had just done. I felt so grateful for my legs that kept moving, my strength that carried me through the tough miles, and a body that got me to the finish line without injury.

No, my body may not have looked like the “marathon body” I once imagined, but it was a marathon body. I didn’t need a mirror selfie to prove what I’d accomplished: I ran all 26.2 miles. Honestly, what could be more impressive than that?

Alyssa is a New York City–based runner and lifestyle writer covering the intersection of fitness, beauty, and everyday routines. Currently training for the NYC Marathon, she brings a firsthand perspective to stories on endurance, recovery, and the mental side of running—alongside the real-life habits that support it. Her work is rooted in what’s practical, personal, and worth incorporating into your own routine.

Five Pieces of Jewelry Calder Made for His Family Head to Bonhams With $300 K. Estimate

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Alex Calder is perhaps best known for inventing the mobile—a kinetic sculpture in which abstract shapes are gently moved by natural air currents—but the late, great American sculptor was also a talented jeweler.

In fact, Calder, known to many as “Sandy,” produced approximately 1,800 unique, handcrafted pieces of wearable art throughout his life, according to the Met, which held the first museum exhibition dedicated to his jewelry in 2008. He famously created custom jewelry for his closest family and friends—five pieces of which will go under the gavel at Bonhams this fall. Related Articles In Postwar Rural France, Alexander Calder Found Respite from Pressures of Fame and a Chance to Dream Big How Collectors Jon and Kim Shirley Stepped In to Restore the Senate Building's Famed Calder

Calder made jewelry as bold and dynamic as his sculpture, constantly experimenting with materials and forms. His avant-garde creations, often in brass, copper, and silver, became popular with 20th-century high society, artists, and celebrities, including socialite Peggy Guggenheim, painter Georgia O’Keeffe, and actress Anjelica Huston, to name but a few.

Calder’s jewelry continues to resonate with high-profile names, too: Just this past September, reality TV star-turned-model Kendall Jenner wore a pair of his hand-hammered silver earrings at the Venice Film Festival. (They originally belonged to the wife of renowned graphic designer Ivan Chermayeff, according to Louisa Guinness, the London-based gallerist who gave them to Jenner.)

The five pieces heading to auction were each made as a gift for a woman in Calder’s family: his sister, his mother, and his niece. They are entirely bespoke, made for that particular person. Because Calder refused every offer to mass-produce his jewelry, any piece that comes to market is a rare find. This quintet has remained in private hands for nearly 70 years, making it rarer still.

The auction star is a necklace Calder made for his sister Margaret Calder Hayes. “Peggy” was a close confidante, a lifelong muse, and a recipient of his early works. Though it was executed circa 1940, the silver piece showcases hand-wrought curves that feel decidedly modern. It is expected to hammer down for between $100,000 and $150,000.

Also on offer are sculptural silver earrings (estimate: $40,000–$60,000) that Calder gifted Peggy, which look very similar to the pair Jenner wore at Venice. Rounding out the five are two silver brooches spelling out the initials of Calder’s mother (Nanette) and niece (Eva), and a spiral-shaped silver ring for his niece.

Bonhams has valued the collection at nearly $300,000. It will be on view at the auction house’s new N.Y.C. flagship from November 7, before crossing the block in the Post-War & Contemporary Art sale on November 19.

Free Stock Is Real Money – Use It to Break a Tie Between Brokers

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Free stock from a broker is real money, but a sign-up share is often worth a few dollars. The broker you pick may hold your investments for decades. Shortlist online brokers by their costs, the funds they sell, and the account you need, then take a free-stock offer only from a broker on your shortlist.

A sign-up share pays you once. The broker’s fees and choice of funds affect you every year you stay, and any charge for moving your account out comes due the day you leave.

You can get free stock four ways. Brokers give shares to new customers who open or fund an account, as Webull’s free-share offer does, and to current customers who refer friends. Stash’s Stock-Back debit card pays rewards in stock, and Acorns invests the rewards you earn shopping with its partner brands.

The shares tend to be small, and they come with conditions. Robinhood’s sign-up stock is worth $5 to $200, and about 99% of new customers get the minimum. You can sell the share after 3 trading days, but you can’t withdraw its cash value for 60 days. It also comes only with an individual taxable account, not an individual retirement account (IRA). Any tax on it is yours, and its value may show up on a 1099 tax form.

Skipping that order can turn a free share into a loss. Say you take an $8 share from a broker that doesn’t carry the funds you want and charges $75 to transfer an account out. Paying the fee to move the account leaves you $67 behind. A fractional free share, a slice of one share, can’t transfer to another broker, the brokerage regulator FINRA notes, so you’d have to sell it first.

Take a free share only from a broker you’d have opened without it.

Find that broker among the best online brokers for beginners.

A Frightening Encounter on a Dark Road Nearly Took Night Running From Me. This Year, I’m Taking It Back.

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I N THE DARK, the headlights cast a distorted version of my silhouette on the industrial building to my left as the vehicle makes its way past me. I’m running along an otherwise desolate road, not far from my house. The car comes to a halt at the stop sign about 300 feet in front of me—and idles. My legs instinctively slow down, my body reacting before my mind has a chance to catch up. I quickly calculate what to do next. There are no side streets I can turn into—I either have to run past the car or turn around, which would add a few miles to my run that I’m not ready for. Maybe the driver is just checking his phone, I tell myself.

But the broad-shouldered man with a long graying beard at the wheel is waiting for me with the window rolled down, his arm resting in its frame. He starts shouting before I can make sense of the situation: What do I think I am doing, running in the dark? Am I trying to get killed? My body tenses, anticipating the man’s possible next move. He has daughters my age, he continues, and he’d kill them if they did that. Could I at least carry this with me, he roars, throwing a key-chain alarm to my feet, then driving off before I have a chance to react.

I sprint the last half a mile home, propelled by anxious energy. My inability to focus only fuels my confusion; so does the blue egg-shaped alarm in my trembling hand that I don’t have a clear memory of picking up but am holding nevertheless. I try to replay in my head how it got there—and why. I have reflective bands on both my arms and my ankles, so drivers can spot me. I use my phone’s light to illuminate the ground in front of me whenever I need to.

The next day, I don’t run outside in the dark. Too cold, I convince myself when I step on the treadmill I hate so much in my apartment complex gym. Too rattled, is what I am unwilling to admit. Because do I have any right to feel that way when technically nothing happened to me? The stranger, in the end, didn’t hurt me.

Still, something changed on the road in the dark.

With every rhythmic thump the belt makes, I am less determined to keep going. My mind can’t handle the monotony. I start skipping days when I cannot make it out for a daytime run. At some point over the next few weeks, the key to the gym falls behind the shoe rack, and I don’t even notice.

THE LIKELY DIAGNOSIS is seasonal affective disorder. It could also be winter blues, but that one is milder and more temporary. My somber mood and low energy seem constant. For most people, it passes by spring, my therapist says. What helps? For one, exercise! But at that point, my couch’s cushions have resigned to accepting my body’s indentation as their new shape.

By the time spring comes, my fitness is at odds with my enthusiasm for the prolonged daylight, and I end up with shin splints. I spend the rest of the year trying to heal and return to form.

I don’t forget about the gray-bearded man. In fact, I share the story, framing it as a silly encounter. “Would you believe that?” I say with a forced smile.

“That’s not okay,” my friends and coworkers answer with their words as much as their faces.

My feelings start morphing from self-blame into anger. What should have been clear to me from the beginning dawns on me slowly: Vigilance is important, but the solution to a stranger’s concerns about my safety is not to instill more fear in the name of “protection.” Women running at night are not the problem. We’ve long ago dismantled the patriarchal notion that men should be our protectors when, in fact, they often pose the danger.

And so when the clock falls back an hour at the start of November, I’m determined not to fall back into a cycle of seasonal depression and injury.

BY MID-DECEMBER, it’s dark by 4:30 p.m. I wrap my torso in color-changing LED tubes. Today I pick emerald. As I descend the trail that clings to the bank of the Delaware River, the historic cantilever bridge that crosses the water comes into view, itself wrapped in green color-changing lights. I smile for no other reason but that we match.

I run up a semicircle road where, for a moment, the world is reduced to cones of warm yellow glowing from vintage streetlights. My chest lamp illuminates just enough ground in front of me to fit my next two steps. Beyond that, I trust that my feet remember what my eyes cannot see.

The darkness has a way of taming my unruly mind. The stillness mutes my whirling thoughts, a nearly impossible achievement among the hectic noise of daylight. It’s the closest I come to meditating, to understanding what it means to be present. It doesn’t matter where the road leads or how long is left of my run; it’s only here and now. The quiet is mostly disrupted by my own two feet. They take turns striking the ground, but their thuds meet in the echo that reverberates off building walls before getting swallowed up by the endless nightfall blue encircling me.

How did I ever allow this to be taken away from me?

Someone lingers at the end of the sidewalk in front of me, and I instinctively switch to the opposite side, picking up speed.

The person pays me no mind, looking in the direction of a leash they’re holding, the end of which I cannot make out. The black road becomes deserted again, and I slow my pace. What was born out of necessity has grown into my favorite part of the day. I love this. I need this. This is the world I want to live in.

This article originally appeared in the Spring 2026 issue of Runner’s World.

Pavlína Černá, an RRCA-certified run coach and cycling enthusiast, has been with Runner’s World, Bicycling, and Popular Mechanics since August 2021. When she doesn’t edit, she writes; when she doesn’t write, she reads or translates. In whatever time she has left, you can find her outside running, riding, or roller-skating to the beat of one of the many audiobooks on her TBL list.

A Pediatrician, a Pastor, and a Paralympian Just Did Wonders for Kids’ Mental Health

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

— The midterm elections offer lessons in decency and difference

My son is 32. Early Wednesday morning he forwarded me a New York Times story about the new faces Tuesday's midterm elections sent to Washington and to statehouses.

He added a note: "Some themes for your next article, like the Knicks one. A message about being decent human beings. A message about how what makes us different brings us together." Then he quoted one line from the Times article: "There's a pediatrician, a pastor, a Paralympian and a former Navy helicopter pilot."

In June, I wrote that the Knicks' championship handed parents a teaching moment. My son was right: Tuesday handed us another.

Our kids and pediatric patients need the lesson. They have lived through a full campaign season. Attack ads. Comment sections. Adults yelling at screens. Before the last presidential election, 77% of American adults named the future of the nation a significant source of stress. Children absorb what we carry home. In the Trevor Project's 2024 national survey, 90% of LGBTQ+ young people said politics had hurt their well-being in the last year. Let's call that the un-teaching.

First, start with the line my son quoted. The pediatrician is Annie Andrews, MD, of South Carolina. She ran for the Senate on a platform focused on children's health after a 2025 measles outbreak sickened some 1,000 children in her state. Andrews, a pediatric hospitalist, saw harm to kids and asked for the job of stopping it.

The pastor is Adam Hamilton, who leads the largest United Methodist congregation in the country. In January he was a registered Republican. Now he is the first Democrat to win a New Hampshire Senate seat in Kansas since 1932. He campaigned on his faith and promised to work across the aisle.

The Paralympian is Josh Turek of Iowa. Two gold medals in wheelchair basketball. A working-class kid. A man who once voted for George W. Bush. He is headed to the Senate.

The helicopter pilot is Rebecca Bennett. She flew for the Navy, ran a healthcare business, and now heads to the House from New Jersey.

Then come the firsts. Keisha Lance Bottoms of Georgia will be the first Black woman ever elected governor in this country. Chris Pappas of New Hampshire will be the first openly gay man in the Senate; he told the Times he is living a life he could not have pictured as a boy. Abdul El-Sayed, MD, DPhil, of Michigan, a physician who ran Detroit's health department, will be the first Muslim senator. Melat Kiros, 29, becomes the first Gen Z woman elected to Congress. James Talarico, a former middle-school teacher who quotes Scripture in his stump speech, will be the youngest member of the Senate at 37.

Ask a child what these people share. Different faiths. Different bodies. Different races, ages, loves, hometowns. Each one walked into rooms full of strangers and asked for their trust. The strangers said yes. What makes each of us different brought them together.

Talk about Amy Acton, MD, MPH. A physician, Ohio's former health director, now its governor-elect. She has spoken openly about a traumatic childhood and sexual abuse at the hand of her stepfather. Late in the race, an ad from her opponent put Acton's estranged mother on camera to call her a liar. Acton held her ground. She said she told her story so other people might see a way out.

As a child psychiatrist, I treat children with histories like hers. Most of them believe the worst thing that happened to them is the truest thing about them. Let's tell these patients Acton's story.

Adolescents build an identity partly by borrowing one. They pick role models whose life stories connect to their own, and they gravitate toward role models who resemble them. For some kids that is a matter of life and death. LGBTQ+ youth who have an older LGBTQ+ role model report lower rates of considering and attempting suicide. In an earlier Trevor survey, more than 80% said positive public role models made them feel better about who they are. A gay teenager in Manchester watched a gay man win a New Hampshire Senate seat Tuesday night. A kid in a wheelchair in Des Moines watched a Paralympian win one in Iowa. That registers.

Difference, met up close, heals. Cross-group friendships rank among the strongest known antidotes to prejudice in childhood, and they support children's well-being. Civic life protects too. In a national study that followed more than 9,000 adolescents into adulthood, those who volunteered and voted went on to have better mental health and engage in fewer risky behaviors. Community engagement in adolescence predicted fewer depressive symptoms years later.

My son also pointed out another crucial point: Kids across the country just witnessed an incredible underdog story. Talarico and El-Sayed were up against hundreds of millions of dollars in opposition spending. Several of the winning candidates represent groups historically left out from Congress, including gays, Blacks, Hispanics, and women. There is something immensely powerful about marginalized candidates running campaigns built around taking care of their communities and defeating the wealthiest individuals in the country. It doesn't matter how powerful you are, compassion for others and belief in self is what can change the world. That's a story our young patients need to witness.

Of course, half of the country may have woken up disappointed Wednesday. But even for that half, the lesson holds. Hamilton was a Republican 10 months ago. Turek voted for Bush. A child can admire a person and still argue with the platform. Disagreement is healthy. Contempt is the toxin. After the last election, the head of the American Psychological Association warned that pulling away from people over politics can cost us the relationships we need most.

Decency. Service. Faith worn in the open. Grit in a wheelchair. Honesty about one's own pain. A hand across the aisle. Strangers saying yes to someone unlike themselves.

There's the message. Everyone in this country has a reason to bring it up this week, and a son or daughter or patient will be ready to hear it.

My son is 32. He still sends me lessons. That's how it's supposed to work. Don't let the moment pass.
